"Doubled frames, staggered, so a ram that breaks one breaks only the one. He had them off the *Concord*."

His father's hand stopped, with a shell in it.

"At the Teeth," said Marcus. "He saw how she was built, where she lies, and drew it that night. He says the new one will last a hundred years."

He did not say the rest of what Gaius had said. *If nobody burns her.*

The king laid the shell in its place in the row.

"Has she a name?"

"Not yet." Marcus looked at the shells, and not at him. "She's the crown's. The crown names its own."

His father turned his head to the window, and did not say anything; and Marcus looked with him.

The royal harbor lay below them black between its moles, with the warm canals coming down out of the city through the frost and smoking where they met the sea, and a rim of ice along the quays where they did not. Past the galleys at their moorings, along the foot of the hill, the old berths ran away into the dark with their rusted rings, and their slipways climbing behind them under a skin of snow. In the first of them, nearest the hill, a lantern burned at the taffrail of a black ship. Out beyond the moles the Grey lay across the sea where the world stopped, pale in the dark.

On the sill, where his father could reach it from his chair, lay a length of carved stern-rail off one of his grandfather's lost ships, with the crowned dolphin cut deep in its face. Marcus had last seen it at Cyrene, going into an oilskin in its sea-grass with a brown book and a letter in his own hand. There was no dust on it.

"She's had the last of the fort's iron," he said. "The bloom the garrison left in the undercroft. The smith drew it for her rudder-irons, and for the nails Gaius can't do with treenails, and that was the end of it. After her, Gaius says, everything waits on iron."

"And the next."

"Waits. He wants more iron for every hull after her than Cyrene has seen since the war began. Tough iron, not hard. The kind that forgives you, he says."

"How many hulls?"

"As many as you've berths for."

His father looked down at the dark row under the hill.

"Thirty," he said.

Marcus knew what his father counted from this window. He had known it since he was a boy, without ever being told, and had never heard him say the figure aloud. It was not this one. Then he looked where his father was looking, at the first berth under the hill and the lantern burning in it, and saw which berth had been counted out.

"Where does iron come from, Prefect?"

"Out of a mountain. Not one of ours."

"Whose?"

"Stoneheart's. Gaius says there's no other iron in the isles worth the name, and he's tried most of it."

"Stoneheart," said the king, "asked this house for bread twice, in famine winters. Both times this house sent it a letter." He took up the next mussel. "On excellent paper."

"Then they'll want more than paper."

"Yes."

Stoneheart lay a long way beyond Cyrene's waters. The council's question had come up the stair behind him, and it was sitting at this table now between the pot and the bread, and his father was eating round it.

"The council would like to know who gave me leave," he said.

"I heard them."

"You weren't there."

"No."

Marcus waited. Across the room the writing table stood where the candlelight ended, with its papers squared, and its right-hand drawer shut. Valeria had told him at Cyrene what lay in that drawer, with a space at its foot for a name. He looked at the drawer, and back at the pot. His father pinched a mussel out of its shell, and ate it, and laid the shell in the row.

"Eat your herring," said the king.

Marcus ate his herring. It had gone cold, and was better cold; the salt had come up through it. The frost had grown on the inside of the glass while they talked, in long white ferns, and somewhere down in the city a bell rang the change of the watch, and stopped.

"I had the captain up yesterday," said his father.

"I know."

"What did he tell you?"

"'He told me.'" Marcus put the bread down. "Then he went down the hill to the harbor."

The king considered that. "He said a good deal more to me."

Marcus did not ask what. It had been the right order, the captain first. He had known that on the black beach at Cyrene, holding the standing part of a rope while an old man spliced it in the dark. He had left the telling to the king, because the warrant had been the king's. He minded the order anyway, and was ashamed of minding, and minded.

His father laid down the shell he was holding. Not in the row: by itself, on the bare wood.

"You wrote," he said, "that I sent you there to die."

At the rail, with the Grey going by, Marcus had decided only that he would not be the one to speak of the letter first. He had not decided anything about the other way round.

Under his shirt the oilskin lay where it always lay, against his ribs, and in it, under the rest, a paper in his brother's hand, written at the far end of his father's own council table, that his father had never read. He did not take it out.

"Yes," he said.

The king looked at him across the table. The candle was at his shoulder and the fire behind him, and his face was in shadow; and his voice, when it came, was the one he had used for the barley.

"When I said it," said the king, "I meant it."

Marcus had braced on the stair for a great many things. Not for that.

Down in the harbor a loose block knocked against a mast in the frost, and went on knocking.

He looked at the black shells along the edge of the table, each one sitting in the one before it, square.

"So did I," he said.

His father's hand had been going out toward the pot. It stopped above it, and stayed there.

He looked at Marcus for a long time. Then his eyes moved: up, and a little to one side, and stayed. Marcus thought at first it was the scar, the old bad stitching from behind his left ear to the hinge of his jaw, that his father had never once asked about. It was not the scar. It was higher. It was his temple, where Alana had found the grey that morning in the first of the light: one thread of it among the dark, which she had drawn out from the rest between finger and thumb and held up to the window, and looked at hard, and let go again where it grew.

His father looked at it, and went on looking; and then he saw Marcus see him, and turned his face away to the window, and the berths, and the one lantern burning in the first of them.

Marcus did not know why. He put his hand up to his temple, as if there might be something there a man could feel. There was nothing. Only his hair.

---

The bells began before it was light: the Hall of Kings first, up on the hill, and then every other bell in the city after it. Nobody had died. Nothing had been won. It was the day before Midwinter, and the city rang its bells for the season, and because it could.

Marcus lay under the roof and listened to them. The room was cold enough to make his teeth ache. The only stove in the house that worked was at the bottom of it. The warmth of it came up the stairwell with the smell of fish oil and mutton-bone broth, and gave out somewhere about the second landing. The window under the eaves had iced over on the inside in the night, and the light through it was white and thick.

Alana was sitting up beside him with the blanket round her shoulders and the weather-book on her knees, writing the frost into it.

She had not asked him last night. He had come down off the hill late, and up through the house past Valeria on the landing and over the rotten tread, and she had been awake, and had not asked. She had moved over in the narrow bed to give him the warm place, and that was all.

"What did he say?" she said now.

There had been a great deal said at that table, most of it about barley. Marcus thought about it.

"He said eat your herring."

Her pen stopped. She looked at him over the top of the book for some time, as if he were a sum that had come out right by a method she did not trust.

"And did you?"

"Yes."

"Then I approve of him," said Alana, and went back to the frost.

He lay and watched her at it. She had scraped a hole in the ice on the glass with her thumbnail before he woke, to see the harbor through, and she was setting it all down: the ice on the glass and how thick, and the wind, which was none, and the smoke off the canals, which went straight up.

"There was wine," he said. "He had it taken out."

"Mm," said Alana. She blotted the line. "Your feet have been on the cold grounds. Keep them on your side."

He kept them on his side.

When he got up, the boards took the warmth out of the soles of his feet. He got into his shirt, and the oilskin went in under it against his ribs, stiff with the night's cold, and he stood at the window and put his eye to her hole in the ice.

"Does it show?" he said.

She did not ask what. She looked up from the book at his temple, and took her time over it.

"If you know where to look."

"He knew where to look."

Alana said nothing to that. She reached up from the bed and put his hair straight over it with her fingers, as if it had only been out of its place, and went back to the book.

Through the hole in the ice the harbor lay grey and still, with a rim of new ice along the quays and the canals coming down into it all along the waterfront under their own smoke. Across the water the Old Hill went up white: the terraces of the tombs, and the dome, and the long wall of the palace along the top, and in it the king's tall window, dark. From here it was a chip of glass in a long wall. He had sat behind it last night and eaten herring.

Below him the Fishmarket Steps went down to the water, house on house, with a bough of pine nailed over every door and smoke going up from every chimney; and at the foot of them Felix's mother was already at her board on the bottom step, in a shawl, among the eels, shouting at somebody about a fish.

Valeria knocked and opened the door in the same movement, which was how she knocked, and let Felix in past her with a loaf in a cloth, after she had looked at the loaf.

"From my mother, Highness," said Felix. He unwrapped it on the end of the bed: the Midwinter loaf, dark and heavy, the crust split, and the figs showing in the splits. "She says it's to be eaten hot."

Alana put the back of her hand to it. It was not hot.

"Strictly," said Felix, "it was hot on the Steps."

Alana broke it and gave Marcus the larger part. It was still warm at the heart, sour and sweet at once, and the figs in it were hot enough to burn his tongue; he burned it, and ate the rest anyway, standing at the window in his shirt.

"Mother says the second landing's going up the hill tomorrow night," said Felix. "The lost islands always go, at Midwinter. They give them the bottom table by the doors, and they eat the king's geese, and they come home with the ends in their aprons." He considered it. "Nobody's supposed to take the ends."

"I'm going up with them," said Alana. "They asked me."

She said it lightly, writing. Marcus looked at her. On the night they came in she had gone past the door on the second landing as quietly as she could go; in the morning she had knocked on it with a basket on her arm, and come back up at dark with her eyes red at the rims, and he had shut the door of this room and asked her nothing. Now the women behind that door had asked her up the hill with them, to sit among them in their black.

He did not say anything about that. It was not his to say.

"Then I'll know where you are," he said.

"You'll know where I am." She dipped the pen. "Not the nailed boots, Marcus. Not on that floor."

Felix found something to look at out of the window. Below them on the second landing a door opened, and somebody laughed, and somebody else told her to hush, and laughed too; and the bells, which had stopped, began again.

---

The snow had come on at dusk. The lanes of the Old Hill were white under it, and squeaked, and the cold had got in under Marcus's coat on the way up. Then the porters opened the doors of the Lesser Hall, and the heat came out to meet him.

It was the heat of a hall packed to its walls with people in their best wool and their furs, with the snow going to water on their shoulders; of lamps in every bracket; of the fires built up roaring at either side. It smelled of goose fat and lamp-oil and pine smoke, and of a great many cloaks steaming. His face, which had been numb since the palace gate, began to burn.

At Midwinter, as at a funeral, nobody went armed in the Lesser Hall but the king's guard, and the porters took steel at the door. Marcus gave up his sword. Valeria gave up her knife and then the Farrier, and she came in at Marcus's shoulder in her mail with her right hand empty. He saw her thumb go along the side of her finger, looking for the haft, and stop.

They had mended the hall.

He had not been in it since the night of the funeral, and he had not been in a state, that night, to see much of it. He saw it now. The high table stood across the top of the hall on its dais with a new top to it, pale oak, planed and waxed, among the old dark wood; he had run a knight down the length of the old one, through the salt and the bread and off the end. The screen over the servants' passage, which the knight had gone into, hung on new hinges. Down by the great doors the lowest table was new from end to end. He had gone through the old one. And on the high table, at the king's end, stood the iron candlestick that Roderic had blown out and taken up that night and bent on a fire iron, straightened since by some smith, with a kink in its stem the smith had not been able to get out. Its candles were lit. That night they had been lit for Titus.

They had swept the rushes back from the middle of the floor, for the dance, and the boards between the tables shone.

The king's old steward met him at the foot of the dais and took him up to the heir's place, at the right hand of the king's empty chair. It had been Titus's. Marcus had always sat below it, among the lords' sons. He sat in it now.

There was water in his cup.

Nobody had poured it while he watched. Felix stood behind his chair in a clean shirt with a napkin over his arm, as he had at palace feasts for years, and was looking at the ceiling.

"Cousin," said Damien.

He had come along behind the high table from the king's chair: slender, in good grey wool, with his hands open and his face glad, the king's nephew and the last of the Vaels, the man in the palace whom people trusted at once. He bent his head to Marcus's so as not to be heard down the table.

"I've put the lady of Corvus with her own people," he said. "Down by the doors, with the lost islands. I thought she'd rather." He smiled. "If I've got it wrong, say so, and I'll move half the hall."

It was a kindness. Marcus thanked him for it, and meant it; and Damien moved back along the table to wait by the king's chair.

Marcus looked down the hall for her.

She was at the lowest table, by the doors, at the end nearest the wall, among the women of the second landing in their black; and she was in violet. Not the gown she had walked this hall in as a girl; that had burned with Corvus. It was the good violet she had brought north from Cyrene, laced close, and among all that black it was the only color at the table.

The court was looking at her. He watched it from the top of the hall: heads going round on the lords' benches, a woman in white fox leaning to the woman beside her, a man at the Marshal's table saying something into his cup. They knew who she was. She was the Corvus girl who had walked the length of this hall in violet and been looked through; who had sailed south with the drunk prince to watch him die, and been taken by Tharsis at the Teeth, and brought home again. The quays had a song about her, Felix said, and it was wrong in every verse.

She looked back at them. She did it a face at a time, without hurry: the lords' benches, and the Marshal's table, and the woman in white fox, moving on from each when she had done with it. As a girl she had walked this hall with her chin up over her gown like a banner over a wall. When she had done with the court of Aeridor she turned back to the woman beside her, and went on talking.

Then the porters threw the great doors open, and the cold came in, and the king.

The hall got up. It got up all at once, benches going back across the boards and a cup going over somewhere, and stood. Marcus stood with it at the high table and looked down the length of the hall at his father in the doorway.

The last time he had seen him in that doorway the hall had been wrecked round them both, and Marcus had been standing in the middle of it with his own blood in his mouth; and his father had looked at him across it and sentenced him, in the voice of the throne and not of a father. Marcus had not seen him in a hall since. The king had not come down even for the council.

He came up the hall now between the tables, slowly, with his guard behind him and the hall on its feet on either side, and the snow still on the shoulders of his coat. There was nothing on him but plain dark wool, the thin gold circlet, and his white hair under it. He climbed onto the dais by the steps at the end, with a hand on the rail, and stood at his chair.

The herald came out from the end of the high table, a little old man in a tabard with a paper in his hand and a voice too big for him.

"*Midwinter!*" he cried.

The matters laid before the king in the year were read in the Lesser Hall on Midwinter night, before the meat, to be answered there or left; it was the old way. In Marcus's time his father had always left them, and answered through clerks in the new year, or not at all. The herald unrolled his paper and read the council's matter in the chancery form.

*The Council of the Realm, sitting in the King's name, lays before the King the matter of the Prefect of Cyrene: that he took the King's ships beyond the waters of Cyrene; that, being asked by the Council who gave him leave, he answered,* Nobody; *and that his captain answered,* He sailed without leave. I went with him. *The Council awaits the King's pleasure.*

He rolled the paper up. The hall stood and waited for the king to sit and the meat to come in, as it always had.

The king put his hand inside his coat and took out a folded paper.

For a heartbeat Marcus was back in the small room at the top of the stair, looking at the right-hand drawer of the writing table, and the paper in it with a space at its foot for a name. At the end of the dais, where she stood against the wall, Valeria's hand went flat to her breastbone, over the Marshal's letter, and stayed there.

The king unfolded the paper on the new oak in front of him, and held out his hand without looking, and the steward put his own pen into it, wet.

There were words on the paper already. Marcus, at his right hand, could see them: two words, in his father's own hand, cramped and slow and old, the letters leaning on one another, and the ink of them long dry. He knew that hand. He had a letter in it against his ribs.

His father bent and wrote his name under them, and the day under that. The name went on black and wet under them, and shone in the candlelight. The two words did not.

Whenever they had been written, it had not been tonight. Marcus did not know when.

The king gave the paper to the herald. The herald looked at it, and turned it over to look at the back, where there was nothing, and turned it to the front again and held it nearer the candles, as if the rest of it might come up in the heat. Then he drew breath and gave it to the hall in his great voice.

"*Leave granted.*"

For a moment nothing happened in the hall. Then a sound went down it that was not quite a cheer, from the top to the bottom, and stopped at the doors.

The king did not sit. He stood at his place with his fingers on the table, and spoke to the hall in the voice he had used at the top of his stair for the barley, which was no voice for a hall. The hall went quiet to hear it.

"The Prefect of Cyrene has my leave to take my ships beyond the waters of Cyrene," said the king, "he and such of his officers as sail with him. The war wants iron. Stoneheart has it."

He sat. The hall sat after him, in a long scrape of benches, and nobody in it said anything.

Down the high table the Marshal got to his feet, broad and grey in his ribbons. He looked at the king, and at Marcus, and then at nothing; and he pressed the bull's head of his ring into the ball of his thumb, hard, and took it away.

"The council has His Majesty's answer," said the Lord Marshal. "It will be entered as His Majesty gave it."

He sat down.

At the edge of Marcus's eye Valeria's hand came down from her breastbone, and did not go back.

The herald laid the paper in front of the king, and the king moved it along the new oak until it lay in front of Marcus.

"Majesty," said Marcus.

It was all he said. It was a form, and he was glad to have it.

Then the screen opened and the meat came in. Geese to the high table on boards, the skins crackling and the fat running off them into the wood; and after the geese a boar's shoulder, carried up on a plank between the boys. Down the low tables went the mutton, swimming in its fat. The Midwinter loaf was on every board, and the black Vessan went round in its jugs.

Marcus ate goose. The skin broke under his teeth, and the fat ran out of it hot and salt, and he wiped it up with the loaf. The jug of Vessan came down the high table toward him, and he smelled it come, plums and pitch. The boy with it saw the water in his cup, and passed him to the next place without a check.

All down the hall the court watched his cup. He could feel it watching, from the lords' benches and the Marshal's table and the daughters near the dais, to see whether it would change.

He let it watch.

---

They cleared the boards of the geese, and the boys came round with the loaf and the last of the Vessan, and up in the gallery over the screen the musicians began to tune. Then they stopped tuning, and the hall fell quiet. It knew what came next.

By custom the king's house opened the Midwinter dance. The heir led out the Long Measure, which had been Corvus's before it was the court's, and danced its opening figure with whoever he chose while the hall watched; and after that the hall took the floor. Titus had always led it out. He had danced it as he did everything, well and without seeming to try, with whichever lord's daughter the seating had put nearest the dais, and handed her back to her father at the end with a bow that made the father glad he had come.

There was no Titus this Midwinter. The hall sat with its cups in its hands and waited to see what the heir would do instead.

The seating had seen to that too. The daughters of the great houses sat along the tables nearest the dais in a bright row, in blue and green and russet, with their hair dressed high and their mothers beside them, where the heir could not miss them. A good many of them were looking at him now.

Marcus put down his cup and stood up.

"The tables are new," said his father, beside him, without turning his head.

Marcus turned to him. The king was looking out over the hall with his face as it always was, and there was no telling from it whether that had been a joke, or a warning, or only a thing about tables.

"Yes, Majesty," said Marcus.

He stepped down off the dais. Behind him he heard Valeria's mail as she left the wall to follow.

"Stand easy, Captain," he said, without turning round.

The mail stopped.

He went down the hall.

The daughters' tables were nearest, and he passed them close enough to smell the orris-root in their hair. He felt the bright row of them watch him come and go by, and the mothers see it a moment after the daughters did. The herald's daughters sat together at the end of a bench beside their little old father, fair, and much alike. Somewhere among them was the girl he had turned to on a Midwinter night in this hall, when a girl in violet had walked its length toward him in a gown she had saved for, and he had looked straight through her and smiled past her at the herald's daughter, because the eyes of his friends were on him and he had wanted to be the sort of man who did not need to be walked to. He looked at their faces in passing. He could not have said which of them she was. They were all looking at him.

Now he walked it. The whole length of the hall, in front of all of them.

He went down between the long tables in the heat of the fires. Lord Sestius turned on his bench to watch him go, with the badge of his lost island on his collar; young Opimius said something into his new fur and was not answered. At the Marshal's table Ursus sat at the end of the bench with his red beard on his chest and his nose to one side. He drew his great boots in under the board as the prince went by, which Marcus had not looked for. Then the guild-masters of the city in their chains, and the masters of the galleys, and a table of priests who had stopped eating. The fire on the left put its heat on that side of his face, and then the fire on the right on the other. A dog came out from under a bench to look at him, and thought better of it.

He did not hurry, and he did not count. The floor was as long as it was.

Behind him the hall had gone quiet. He could hear it being quiet: the fires, and a lamp spitting in its bracket, and the rustle of a great many people turning round on their benches to see where he was going, and, small and distinct, somebody setting down a cup.

The lowest table stood across the foot of the hall by the great doors, where the draught came in under them, and the lost islands sat at it: old men from Thessa with their caps on the board beside them; a family from somewhere further west in their good clothes, with a baby asleep on the bench; and at the end by the wall the Corvus women from the lodging-house in their black, with their heads together, and among them, with her back to him, in violet, Alana, talking.

She did not turn round. She had seen him coming. He knew it by the back of her neck, under the pinned hair. She was telling the woman beside her something, a small grey woman in black, and she finished telling it, while the prince of Aeridor stood at her shoulder before the court with nothing to do but wait. The woman heard her out, and nodded, and raised her eyes to him.

Then Alana got up, and turned on her heel toward the floor, and lifted her right hand, palm down, to the height of her shoulder, and held it out into the place where the man should stand.

He laid the back of his wrist under her fingers, as the court does it.

They went out onto the floor between the tables, and up in the gallery the musicians found the measure, and the hall watched them.

He did not see much of the hall after that. He saw her. The Corvus women had put up her hair for the court, pinned high and close, with a comb of horn in it at the back; the good violet was laced close, and she held her skirts down in her free hand, so that nothing showed of her below the hem but the toes of her shoes on the turns. It was the slowest dance the court had. The walk round, her fingers on his wrist, her eyes on his face. The great turn under his lifted arm, slow, the violet going round her heavy and settling again. The passing, back to back, near enough to feel the warmth of her through his coat, and not touching. He had danced it every Midwinter since he was a boy, with a great many daughters, and had never once looked at any of them while he did it. He did not look at anything else.

Her face was grave through all of it. On the passing he saw the corner of her mouth go, and come back.

Then the last figure; and at the end of it the yard of polished floor that the court keeps between the man and the woman for the bow and the curtsy. He bowed. She sank into her curtsy with her skirts held, and rose out of it, and they stood on either side of the yard with the hall looking at it.

The yard said what it said. Neither of them closed it.

Behind them the music went round again, and the hall came down onto the floor, the lords' sons for the lords' daughters and the guild-masters for their wives, a little late, and talking. Marcus gave Alana his wrist and took her back to her place at the lowest table, at the end by the wall.

She sat. She looked up at him; then past him, up the hall, to the high table, where the heir's place stood empty at the king's right hand; and back at him.

"Back?" said Alana.

He sat down beside her on the end of the bench, with his back to the wall.

The bench was new. The wall was not. It was cold through his coat, and the draught from the great doors came along the floor round his ankles. Under the board, where the court could not see, her knee came against his, and stayed there. On the board in front of him was mutton swimming in its fat, and the end of a goose, and the dark loaf. After a little Felix came down the hall from the high table with the prince's cup of water in his hand, and the court turned on its benches to watch a cup of water go by. He set it down in front of Marcus and went and stood against the wall. Valeria came down after him, along the wall, in no hurry, and took her place where she could see the doors.

"Good ground, Highness," she said.

The small grey woman in black, whose sentence he had waited for, pushed the mutton down the board to him through its fat.

"Eat," she said.

---

**DAMIEN**

The dish came down from the top of the king's stair at the turn of the night, as it had come down every night since the autumn, and Damien was in the king's kitchen when the steward brought it in.

It was a small vaulted room under the palace, apart from the great kitchens below the Lesser Hall. Tonight it was cold. The feast had been cooked below, and the fire here had been let down under its ash; the boys who turned the spits were asleep on the flour sacks in the corner with their mouths open. The room smelled of wet stone and old fat.

Albia sat by the fire with her hands rolled in her apron. She had cooked the king's suppers since before either of the princes was born. She did not get up when the steward came in, because they had been old together too long for that, and she did not get up for Damien, because she never had; he had liked that in her since he was a boy.

The steward set the dish on the board, with the cup beside it, and climbed back up with his lamp without a word.

Damien lifted the cover himself.

The mutton lay as it had gone up, cut thick on its bed of barley, and the fat had set over it white and hard. The cup beside it had grown a skin. It smelled of cloves, and of nothing else.

"He's not touched it," said Albia.

"No," said Damien.

It had been so since the autumn night the black ship came into the first berth. For the first nights Damien had carried them up himself and stood by the writing table, smiling, until his uncle ate; and his uncle had thanked him, and said that he would eat presently, and sent him to bed. Two nights ago the steward had gone down to the galley quay before dark with a basket on his arm, like a cook's boy, and come back up the hill with herring. The kitchens had had it before the herring was cold.

And tonight at the high table Damien had laid the best of the goose on his uncle's plate with his own hands, the breast and the crackling, as he laid it every Midwinter; and his uncle had let it stand, and broken the loaf, and eaten the bread.

Nobody in the hall had seen it; the hall had been watching the prince's cup. But the plates came down to the kitchens, and talk went down a palace as the wine went down a table, from the top, a little thinner at every place, and reached the foot all the same. Before the feast-days were out the scullions would have it, and the lords' cooks, and then the lords. *The king won't eat what his nephew sends up.*

He could not have said why. He had asked, lightly, whether the mutton was not as his uncle liked it; and his uncle had regarded him for a moment as though there were something he was trying to remember and could not reach, and said that it was good mutton, and not eaten it. Damien did not think his uncle could have said why either. That was the part he liked least.

He was fond of the old man. He had come up this hill at seven, the last of the Vaels, in a black coat too big for him, and his uncle had looked down at him from a great height and given him a room with a window over the garden, and a chancery master, and a corner of his own table; and Damien had been sorry about everything ever since, politely, and grateful for all of it. And the old man was thinner. Damien saw it every day, at the wrists and in the cords of the neck. Somebody had to see that he ate.

And in the south, in a fishing town at the edge of the Grey, the Navarch of Tharsis sat in a room over a slip gate and wrote everything down. His books were in the hands of the girl from Corvus, who read everything, and who tonight had danced the Long Measure with the heir of Aeridor, fetched for it from the foot of the hall, where Damien had put her. Somewhere in all that ink there might be a line that concerned a man in Aeridor. There might not.

He had asked his uncle in the autumn, at the right moment and in the right words, to have the Navarch brought north; and his uncle had said, *If the council wants him, the council may ask.* So Damien had given the council its words. He had sat beside young Lord Opimius at Lord Sestius's table in the frosts, and talked to him of horses until the meat, and let fall over the second-best wine that a Navarch of Tharsis ought surely to be questioned by people who knew how. Opimius had said it under the dome as his own. The council had not asked.

The man across the water who bought his hand paid for what it brought him, and a nephew the king had begun to refuse would bring less. Yet that man was the only one since the war began who had looked at the last of the Vaels and asked his price, instead of assuming his answer. Everybody else assumed it: his uncle fondly, and the court without ever thinking about it at all. Tonight the court had watched the king's younger son walk the length of the Lesser Hall to its lowest table, past every daughter Damien had seated for him, and had loved him for it. They had loved Titus for the sword, and they would love this one for the canal and the coming back; and nobody had ever loved a man for a seating chart, though there was not a hall in the isles that could sit down to its meat without one.

If the Navarch could not be brought north, then let Tharsis come south for him.

He saw it all at once, standing over the cold mutton. *Such of his officers as sail with him.* Not *sailed*. Damien had written *sailed* himself, in the autumn, at the corner of the king's table; he had heard the word change tonight, as he heard every word that changed in a form, and admired it. He had been glad for his cousin, and said so, and meant it as far as it went. The Prefect would go west for his iron with his ships and his officers and his famous cohort, and Cyrene would be empty behind him. It wanted only the day; and the letters that came up from the south by every ship would give him the day.

He put the cover back on the mutton, and set it straight, and went up.

His rooms were warm; he had had the fire kept in. In the press by the window lay every seating he had drawn since he was a boy, folded in order, and tonight's on the top of them. It had come out right at every place but the one at the king's right hand. He sat down at his table, and trimmed a pen, and wrote.

He wrote four sheets, close, in the most beautiful hand in the palace, without a blot, every letter leaning the same way. He put the king's words first, because a king's words go first. He wrote, as the packets were always written, of the nephew, in the third person all the way through, as of a man he knew and thought well of.

*Before the court, at Midwinter, the king gave the Prefect of Cyrene leave to take his ships beyond the waters of Cyrene, with such of his officers as sail with him, to Stoneheart, for iron.*

And near the end, where it would be read twice:

*The army will be away. The nephew will send the day.*

He sanded the sheets and folded them, and drew his thumbnail down the fold to set it, as the chancery masters had taught him. The oilskin was in the drawer where it lived. Before the bells it would go down the hill by the hand that always took it, to the salt-boat, which sailed on the night tide in any weather.

Then he took a clean sheet and wrote a character for a cook.

*Albia has cooked for the King's household since before the princes were born, and has given entire satisfaction. She is honest, clean and sober, and her mutton is the best on the Old Hill. She leaves the household at its own wish, and with its thanks.*

He signed it, and folded it, and drew his thumbnail down the fold. He took a purse from his own pocket, and weighed it in his palm, and put another coin into it, and went down.

She was where he had left her, by the fire, with the dish on the board under its cover. The boys had not stirred.

He drew up a stool and sat down across the hearth from her, which nobody from upstairs had ever done, and told her. He told her that His Majesty's tastes had changed with the winter, as the kitchen had seen by the steward's basket, and that the household must make a change in the kitchen to answer them, and that it was nobody's fault, and least of all hers. He gave her the purse, and the paper. He was sorry about this, and he said so in the right words.

Albia looked at the purse in her lap, and at the paper. She turned it over, and studied the hand on it for some time, as people did.

"It's not the mutton, my lord," she said.

"I know," said Damien.

She put the paper into her apron with the purse, and got up, and took her shawl down from the peg by the yard door.

Tomorrow the court would talk of nothing but the prince at the lowest table with the girl from Corvus. Nobody would talk about a cook.

He did not ask her where she would go.

He held the yard door for her himself. It was snowing in the yard, and the lamp over the door lit the snow for a few steps and no further. She put her shawl over her head, and went out of the light.

---

# Chapter Five: Pressure, Applied

**RUSCA**

The flock came off the mountain at first light, one beast at a time, and Rusca counted it.

She counted from the flat rock on the headland above the Shambles, as she had every dawn and dusk since she came, with her back to a boulder and the book open on her knee. The rock was wet, and its cold came up through the Survey's black. The sleet drove in off the sea in ruled lines. Above the cliffs the snow began, and above the snow the high vents breathed their smoke into the morning, yellow-grey and slow; when the wind laid it over the headland she tasted sulphur on her teeth. She had eaten first, barley porridge boiled thick with salt and goat fat, and a cup of hot water with nothing in it; the Survey did not drink in the field. Her mitts left her fingers bare for the pen. She wet her thumb to turn to the day's page, and dried it on the square of linen in her cuff, so that the page would not cockle.

They were not large. That had been her first correction to the Survey's file on Stoneheart. The file was copied out of old Aeridor books, and the old books gave the mountain one dragon the size of a granary, with a grievance. She had found a flock. No beast in it was bigger than a horse, if a horse were mostly neck and tail and opened on either side into sails of skin wider than a hunting boat was long. They roosted in the warmth of the high vents, where the rock never froze, and they were the colors of the mountain: slate and rust, ash and soot, and the grey-green of the lichen on the lower rocks. A beast that held still against the cliff on a wet morning was not there at all until it moved. She had entered forty-three, by their marks.

Each had two lines in her book, because the light was different at either end of the day, and so were the marks. In the morning they came off the vents against the grey, and she knew them by their colors: the soot with the white throat, the rust with the dark saddle, the old slate gone pale along the spine. At dusk they came home against the red of the vents, flat as cut paper, and she knew them by their sails: a notch, a tear, a finger short, the pucker where an old bolt had gone through and healed. The Thessa men had names for a few of them. Hers were figures, Slate One and Rust Four and Lichen Two, and hers were better, because they could be added.

Slate One came off first, as it did most mornings, dropping off the lip of the vent into the smoke and opening there, so that the smoke went round its sails in two long wheels. She ticked it. Then the two soots together, and a rust, and Ash Three with the hitch in its left sail. Each beast dropped, and opened, and turned, and the turn was the figure: a turn down over the headland toward the sea went in the column for the coast, and a turn over the shoulder of the mountain, toward the high pastures where the clans grazed their herds, in the other.

In her first week each tick had gone in the first column. The coast had fed them all then, a short drop from a warm bed: goats on the Shelves, seals on the Skerries, deer in the Hangers. The Shelves were bare now. The Hangers were nearly so, and the Skerries were going. The coast column was falling, and the inland column was rising.

When the last was off she read down the list for the ones without a tick, and there were none; and she read it again, because the first time was a reading, and the second was a count.

*Over the coast: twelve.* She wrote it, and the inland figure beside it, and added the two, and they came to the whole flock, as they ought.

Down on the Skerries the boats were in on the last of the ebb, and the stock had gone over their sides to stand in the wash along the seaward rocks, a dark line with its back to the sea, as she had written it the night before: *Stand in the wash. Let nothing by.* Rust Four had turned for the coast. It came down on the Skerries out of the sleet with its sails half shut, at the far rock the hunters had not reached. The Thessa men ran at it shouting with their clubs up; and one of the stock stood in the wash beside the seal it wanted, as written.

It coughed.

Rusca counted. *Stock in hand, raised—*

It was out before *lost*. A short gout of fire, dull orange in the grey, went out of the beast's jaws and fell on the rock and on the shoulder of the stock, and stopped, as a pen stops that has run dry in the middle of a stroke. The stock's oilskin smoked. It stood. Rust Four took the seal off the rock in its hind feet and went away low over the water toward the cliffs, laboring, and dropped it once, and went down after it, and had it again.

Half a carry.

The carry was hers. At first the hunters had brought her flames that were long, and short, and *a good while*, none of which was a figure; so she had made one. A carry was the time it took to say the heads of a return, *stock in hand, raised, lost, carried*, at the pace the audit was read, with no hurry in it and no weight on the last. Every clerk had that pace in his ear, and neither cold nor fright quickened it. In her first week the same beast had burned four carries over the Skerries, a flame that lay along the water and went on lying there, and would have cost her the stock and very likely the boat. She had a month of figures now, and they went one way. A beast that had eaten burned long. A beast that had not, coughed.

She entered it on Rust Four's line, with the stock beside it: *one, scorched; held.*

The wind brought the smoke down over the headland, and a strand worked loose from the heavy braid wound into a knot at her nape and blew across the page. She tucked it back with the end of her pen. Her hair was the color of a new bronze coin, and on this coast it came loose every day; everything here did that was not tied down twice.

The one on the high vent was not in the count.

It was there this morning, as every morning, on the highest of the vents where the smoke came out thickest: twice the size of Slate One, lying along the rock with its head up, the color of the cliff itself, so that she had looked straight at it for days before she saw it. She had not once seen it come down. It did not hunt the coast, and it did not go over the ridge. It lay in the warmth and watched the flock go; and the coast, and the boats, and whoever sat on the headland with a book.

It was in no column. She had looked for one.

---

The boats came into the Shambles on the young flood, low in the water with the morning's seals, and the stock pulled them in on the count of a clerk in each stern. The Navarchy rowed with the living and was proud of it. The Survey was proud of other things.

The Shambles was a beach of black shingle at the head of a cove below the Chimneys. That was the Thessa men's name for the cliffs below the vents, and it had gone into her book because it was shorter than hers. Under a roof of tarred sailcloth stood the trestles and the brine tubs. The try-pots sat on hearths of loose stone at the top of the beach. By the waterline the casks stood in squared ranks, each with the closed hand burned into its head and a tally beneath it in her writing. The hunters gutted and flensed on the trestles. The meat went into the casks between layers of salt, and the blubber into the try-pots to come out as oil. The stock carried all of it from one place to the next, and set it down, and went back. The smell was brine and blood with the try-pots under both, a sweet rancid smell that got into wool and stayed, so that the boats smelled of it, and Rusca's own cloak.

Not a scrap was left on the shingle that a dragon could use; the offal went out past the Skerries in weighted sacks on each ebb. In the first days the flock had come over to look, and troubled them no more.

She went down to the boats, and the hunters stopped talking when she started walking, which they had learned to do. She did not raise her voice, on a beach or anywhere; she walked to whoever she wanted, and spoke low.

Mastarna was standing in the wash with his boots off and his trousers rolled, counting seals out of the bilges onto the shingle. He was headman of the Thessa men, a grey-bearded cragsman with an old rope-scar round his left wrist as neat as a bracelet, and no use for letters. He gave her the count his way, by the head, bulls and cows apart. She entered it hers.

"There was a cow in that lot heavy with pup," he said. "We're into the breeders. There'll be nothing on the Skerries in the spring."

"There'll be grain in the spring."

He looked up at the cliffs and spat over his left shoulder, which the Thessa men did when they spoke of the flock, and when a row of stock passed them.

"Where I come from," he said, "we leave the breeders."

"Where you come from," said Rusca, "they eat now."

He considered that, standing in the cold water.

"They do," he said. "My grandsons are fat as seals." And he went back to his count.

Laelius brought up the tally from the last stern. He was eighteen and on his first posting, a long-wristed boy from the south shore with a good plain hand. He had come west in the Survey's issue cloak, good wool, unoiled, and not dry since the voyage out. He coughed while he gave her the tally, and turned his face away to do it, as if a cough were a thing the Survey might enter against him. She took the tally, and looked at the cloak, and said nothing about either.

At noon they ate in the lee of the try-pots, with the heat on their faces and the sleet at their backs, the Survey on one side of the fire and the Thessa men on the other. The hunters fried the morning's seal hearts in slices on the lip of a try-pot, in the seals' own fat. They ate them with barley bread baked flat on the hearthstones and their ration of Thessa ale, which the Survey paid for, entered under stores, and did not drink. They had kept Midwinter on a double ration and a song with more verses in it than the ration would bear. The clerks ate the same hearts and bread, and drank hot water. Seal heart was dark and close-grained and tasted of iron and the sea. She had not cared for it the first week and had stopped noticing it by the second; that was as much as could be said for most things in the field.

The Thessa men had been quick to take up the custom of not looking at the stock, and kept their spitting besides. She had asked for stock from the south-shore quarries, not from Thessa, so that no man of Mastarna's need spend a day not looking at a cousin. It had cost her a letter and a week.

Sethre, who was the talker among them, had a figure of his own for the kites. "Not one. All the time we've been here, and they've not touched one of the cold lads."

"A kite knows meat," said Mastarna.

It was in her book already: *The flock does not take stock.* It was one reason the stock could work under the cliffs at all. The other was the half carry she had entered on the headland at first light.

Larth brought the bread round. He was the youngest of the Thessa men and the best of them on a line; the last goat off the Shelves had come down on his rope. He looked at her a moment longer than the bread needed, then at his own hands, and moved on round the fire.

She entered him where he belonged: Larth, cragsman, paid at a man and a quarter; worth, for that look, an hour's harder work in the afternoon if she smiled at him, and nothing if she did not. She did not. The afternoon had work enough in it.

She knew to the ounce what her face was worth. Her father weighed casks on the quay at Lessa, and she had learned at his beam that everything was worth something exactly, and that most people guessed. At home the face had been worth a second audit: every auditor who had her returns added them twice, because a face like hers was taken to have had help with its figures; and they had agreed the first time, every time, and that was the whole of her pride. Her mother called her eyes amber, and the Survey's roll gave them as yellow, which was the Survey all over. The sea had brought out the freckles across her nose and on the backs of her pale hands, which she disliked. The black was another matter. The best tailor in Lessa had cut it high at the collar, for the length of her neck, and long in the line, to show a good figure without breaking one rule of the Survey's dress. It was her one vanity, and she knew it for a weapon as well; so was a pen. She had not used the face to rise. The Survey's books could not be seduced, and that was what she loved best about them.

After the meal Rusca stood by the brine tubs with the book open along her arm and let herself be pleased with the column.

It balanced. It had balanced every week. The salt came out from Tharsis in the casks it went home in. The oil went to the Guild of the Lamps at the guild's price, and there was never enough of that on the north coast; the hides went to the tanners at Lessa, and the meat to the Survey's own stores. The hunters' pay and the salt and the freight came to less than all of it, by a margin she had entered under *carried*, where the Spire would see it. Nothing was killed on this coast that was not eaten. It was only eaten in Tharsis.

At the long table in the Spire, on the morning the Teeth were read, she had known without looking up that the king had added the foot of his page and left it open. Then he had given the Survey its line for the west, *Stoneheart: the stone, by the spring audit*, and had not asked how. A stone by the spring audit would close that page, and she meant the figure that closed it to be hers.

The clans would not sell their stone. They had been asked, and had answered in their own tongue, and then, so that there should be no mistake, in the common one. A mountain full of dwarves with a catapult in every gallery was no place for a war; the clans did not come out to fight in the open, which was the only place a war came cheap; and Tharsis did not pay for a war where a sum would do. Silverwood had been poison and fire, and had failed. Poison could be found, and fire put out. Hunger could not be found anywhere. Hunger was weather.

The mountain had had two hungry winters in living memory, both of them the flock's, and both times the clans had asked the crown of Aeridor for bread and been sent a letter. They had kept both letters, and eaten neither. This would be the third, and the difference was who answered. Thessa had had the same letter once, and had sold a shard of its stone instead, and had eaten every winter since. It was not a new thing she was doing to the mountain. It was an old one, done in good order, with the answer already in the Survey's granaries, docketed for Stoneheart and waiting on her word.

She had put it in two words at the head of every return she sent home, in the place where the form asked for the method.

*Pressure, applied.*

On the evening tide the *Docket* went out past the Skerries, round-bellied, with the week's casks in her hold. She had come in from Tharsis with salt and letters. She went home full of the coast.

---

She ate with the clerks in the *Footing*'s waist that night, goat boiled soft with barley, the last of the Shelves; and afterward, at the table in her cabin, with the tender lifting and settling under her on the swell in the cave mouth, she read her mother's letter.

One came in every hull, and there was a man in every one. This hull's was a deputy of the weigh-house at Lessa, a widower with a good hand and a south window, and inside her column, which was the whole of her mother's case. In Tharsis a marriage was entered with the Survey like a lease, and respectable people married inside their own column. A posting west was a wife's pride, the street said, and she was the youngest quaestor posted west that year. It had been her father's pride, who had told the weigh-house, and her mother's, who had told the street and had wished in each letter since that there were a husband to be proud of it as well. At thirty-two the street had begun to notice that there was not.

She had not gone without. She had only gone quietly, and under one rule: never anyone whose returns she signed, or who signed hers. In Tharsis the bed was nobody's business. The sin was a lover in the ledger.

She had been promised once, at twenty-two, inside her column: a young surveyor of the north shore; a winter cough at his first post; a letter. A month after the letter, the quay at Lessa by night, a file hauling stone, and a walk in it that she knew; and she had not looked at his face, because one did not. A lamp in her window for her year, put out on the day.

Her mother had not written his name since. Rusca answered the letter in three lines, as she answered all of them, the weather and the food and no, and put it at the back of the book, where letters went. On its peg by the door her cloak smelled of the try-pots.

In the morning she wrote the drive.

The stock for the Hangers stood on the shingle at first light in a file, and she walked down the file with her pen turned round in her fingers. The Survey's pen had two ends: a split bronze nib for ink, and a blunt bronze point the size of a barleycorn for the other kind of writing. She did each one the same. The flat of the point once across the brow below the hairline, to smooth out yesterday's sentence; then the new one, in her own neat hand, without hurrying.

*Walk the line. Stop at the water.*

The letters went in under the point, and the skin closed over them as wax closes on a tablet held near a warm hand, and she moved to the next. Close to, the stock smelled of nothing at all. That was the cold. A quaestor wrote on the brow and looked at the letters; it was the first thing the Survey's school taught, and the thing its pupils were slowest to learn, and she had not been slow at it. Some quaestors wrote paragraphs, and wondered why their stock wore through by the spring. The wax only took so much.

The Hangers were the last of the shore woods with anything in them: stunted oak and birch on a slope too steep to stand on, with snow in the hollows and the deer lying up in the thick of it. She put the stock in a line along the top of the wood, a body's length apart, with a clerk behind each half; at the foot the Thessa men strung their nets between the last trees and waited with their short spears. Each half of the line walked on its clerk's count: a figure said, a bead moved, a step taken. She walked behind the middle, where she could see both ends.

The line went straight down into the wood, over the fallen trunks and through the brush, in a smell of wet bark, with the sleet rattling in the dead oak leaves. The stock did not need to see the deer. It did not need to see anything. Ahead of it things began to move: a crack, a brown back going away between the birches, a white rump bounding, then more of them, all going one way, down, toward the water. That was all a drive was: a line that did not stop, and a place where it would.

On the left the count stopped.

She heard it go: Laelius's voice behind the left half, giving a figure and then not giving the next; and then the cough, deep and wet, with a rattle at the bottom of it, that bent him double over his frame. The left half of the line stopped where it stood. It did not fall. It stood among the trees, each one where the last figure had left it, with the sleet settling on its shoulders: a sentence with no one to read it, as the Survey said. The deer found the gap at once. Hinds broke back up through it at a scrambling run, close enough to touch the stock, and went away up the slope toward the cliff.

She was there before they were out of sight. Laelius was down on one knee in the snow with the frame clutched to his chest, trying to give the count between coughs and getting no further than the first sound of it. She took the frame out of his hands, and the count out of the air where he had dropped it, and said the next figure, and moved the bead.

The left half of the line walked.

She kept it walking, and brought the whole line down through the last of the brush and stopped it at the water, as written, with the deer in front of it in the nets. The Thessa men did their work there with the short spear, behind the shoulder. They were quick about it; they were paid by the head.

Then she climbed the slope to Laelius. He was on his feet, grey to the lips and wet through, and shaking with the cold that followed a cough. He began to tell her that he was sorry, Quaestor, that it was the cold, that it would not happen again. The issue cloak hung on him heavy and dark with water.

She unpinned her own. It was a grey sea-cloak of oiled wool, close-woven, from the best sail-loft on the north shore; it had cost her a month's stipend, and the sleet ran off it. She put it round him over his wet one and pinned it at his throat herself.

"Quaestor, I can't take the—"

"You're not taking it. I'm issuing it. You'll sign for it tonight."

He stared at her.

So she smiled at him. It was a good smile, wide, with one eye-tooth that stood a little crooked, and she did not spend it on nothing. She spent it now, because a boy who would not take a cloak from a quaestor might take one from a woman who was smiling at him. He took it. She sent him down to stand by the try-pots until he was warm, with orders to keep the cough on the downwind side of the meat, and she took his half of the line herself through the afternoon's carrying.

She was cold the rest of the day. The black was good cloth, close-cut and lined, and it was not a cloak. The wind off the sea blew through it, and the sleet found the back of her neck below the knot, and her hands went white and then red under the freckles. She kept it out of her shoulders and out of her voice, which were where a beach would have looked for it, and she entered the hinds herself, against the line and not the clerk.

That night Laelius signed for the cloak in the stores book, in a good plain hand that shook, and she countersigned under him in one that did not.

---

Two days after the drive she was back in the Gullet for the afternoon flood.

The *Footing* lay in the mouth of the cave with her masts struck and her bow to the dark, as she had lain since they came, a lean two-masted tender of the Survey. The sea came in under her on the flood and went out on the ebb, and the cave boomed with it day and night, so that in her cabin Rusca felt it through the soles of her boots before she heard it. It smelled of weed and wet stone and tar, and the cold came out of the back of the cave, where the light never reached.

On every flood a file of stock went down the tender's side by the ladder into the black water, one after another, without a sound, and walked away under it toward the back of the cave, where the roof came down to meet the sea; and when the water had closed over the last of them, Blaesus read them on. He kept the deep rows in chalk on a slate, because the damp swelled the wires of any frame he brought into the cave. He was the oldest clerk she had, as bald as a beach stone and about as easy to argue with, and she had not once heard his count stop.

"How are they going?" she said.

"Slowly, Quaestor."

"How slowly?"

"As slowly as yesterday." He made a mark on the slate without looking up. "It's a mountain."

It was. She entered the deep works in their column, *on the flood; slow*, as she entered them every tide. Hunger came down on the mountain out of the air, and was quick, and could be counted from a rock. The deep works were slow, and showed nothing yet, and she had not come west to hurry them. The sea was cold, and the cold kept the stock.

She ate early, in her cabin: the last of the deer livers, fried in the galley with onions until the edges caught, and barley bread, and hot water, which she held in both hands until it was gone. Laelius came aft in her cloak, his cough gone down to a rumor of itself, to ask whether she would have it back. She would not. It was signed for.

The offer lay under the book in its oilskin. She took it out and read it twice.

*To the Forge-Mother of Stoneheart, and to the clans in their halls, from the Survey of the King of Tharsis. The Survey has learned that the clans are in want this winter. It has grain. It will deliver at the mountain gate, on the ——— day of ———, barley and wheat to the weight of the clans' want for the winter, as the clans shall reckon it, weighed on the clans' own scales; against the Heart-Stone of Stoneheart, to be carried home by the Survey's officers with the clans' consent.*

It was in her best hand, on the Survey's good paper. She had the clans' own figures, copied into the Survey's file from the petitions they had sent the crown, and they were very good figures; she would take their reckoning, on their own scales, and no clansman at the gate would have to watch a Tharsian thumb on a beam. The clans measured a grudge in generations. Theirs was with Aeridor, and she meant to leave it there.

Where the date should go she had ruled a space the length of a date, and left it. A clerk did not date a paper before the thing in it was due. The figures would fill it when they were ready.

She put it away, and took up the book, and climbed the headland to count them home.

The wind had gone into the north. There was ice in the rock pools along the path, and it broke under her boots, and the air smelled of snow. She sat on the rock in the black, with her back to the boulder, and kept her fingers moving on the page between figures so that they would go on moving when she needed them. Above the cliffs the vents had begun to glow, a dull red down in the smoke, and against the red the flock came home.

They came in flat and black, and she knew them by their sails. The first came in from the sea, low over the headland, and went up the face of the cliff on the warm air in one long slant: Soot Two, by the tear. She ticked it. Then another from the sea, and two after it. Then none came from the sea for a long while, as the light went; and then they began to come over the ridge.

They came in ones and twos, and one of them came lower than the rest, laboring, with something in its hind feet that was not a seal and was not a goat. It went down on the high vent with it, and the others came down round it. She looked until she was sure. A calf: red, with a white face, off the clans' high pastures. It was the first she had seen with her own eyes.

*Inland: one head of the clans' cattle, taken; seen.*

Once, far off beyond the ridge, she heard the clans' catapults: a knock, and a long while after it another, like a book shut at the far end of a long table. Then nothing.

When the last had come in she read down the dusk lines for the gaps. She read them twice, and then once more, with the end of her pen on each line.

Rust Three had not come home: the dark saddle, the pucker in the right sail. It had gone over the ridge at first light; she had ticked it going. Lichen Two had not come home either, the smallest of them, which had gone over behind it. She watched the glow over the high vent a long while, and no shape crossed it, and the snow came down onto the headland after all, and lay on the page.

Then she entered them.

*Not seen: two.*

The clans had catapults that could reach a beast in the air, and crews who knew how to lay them. The file said so, and the file was right.

On the highest vent the one that was in no column lifted its head as the last of them settled, and looked down the mountain at the headland for a while, and laid its head down again.

*Over the coast: four.*

It was the figure the method had been for since the first day. It had come down in good time, and steadily, with weeks in hand before the spring audit; and she sat with it a moment in the snow, because it was a good figure, and she had made it.

She wrote up the day where she sat, by the red of the vents, with fingers gone slow in the cold; the hand they wrote was the same. The pen sat in the callus on her second finger, and there was no ink on her hands; there never was. Each figure went in its column, and each column was added, and the foot added twice. Under *Remarks* she wrote what the day's figures came to.

*The flock is on the herds. The herds will bear the pressure.*

It was true, and it was the length of the line.

She knew where a flock went when the herds were done. It was in the same petitions. In winter the clans kept their beasts in byres cut into the rock beside the halls, where the vents kept the stone warm, and a flock that had cleared the high pastures would follow the smell of the beasts down into the rock. The petition for the second winter had a column for the byres, and after it a column for the halls, by name and age. She had read it on the voyage out, once, to the foot.

The grain would be at the gate before anyone needed that column again. That was what the space was for.

There was room on the page under *Remarks* for another line. She sanded the one she had written, and wet her thumb, and dried it on the linen in her cuff so that the page would not cockle; and turned it.

---

# Chapter Six: Late Midwinter

**MARCUS**

The *Patience* smelled of figs.

The smell rose through the gratings all the way south: figs in their rush baskets, the brine off the salt beef, the rinds of the cheeses, and from somewhere under the main hatch a sweetness that was the honey. On deck the Grey stank of brimstone. Below, it was harvest.

The stores had come down to the galley quay before light on the morning they sailed, on the king's carts, without a herald. The porters ate their herring first, round the brazier on the quay, with the skins spitting in the coals and the smell of them going up into the dark. Then they wiped their fingers on their thighs and carried the king's stores aboard by lantern-light, in the order the clerk had written them. First went the barley, which was short at Cyrene. Then the salt beef in its casks, and the oil in jars packed round with straw, and the cheeses and the honey and the figs. A few casks of the black Vessan were stowed low and chocked against her ribs, where he did not have to look at them. Last, on top of everything, where the bilge could not reach, went the cloth, in bolts sewn into sacking against the wet.

None of it smelled of cloves.

The king's old steward had come down the hill behind the last cart with his lamp. He put a paper into Avitus's hand, and was gone up the hill again before they sailed. Avitus read it as far as the first fold, and sneezed on it, and begged its pardon. Then he gave it to Alana, since she had kept his stores for him going north and he could see no reason why she should stop.

Valeria was sick before they had cleared the capital's moles, as she had been going north. But since the Lesser Hall her hand had not once gone to her breastbone. On the first evening Avitus came aft along the gangway with his board and his onion and his twist of salt, and stood beside her at the rail, apologizing to the weather. She bit, and salted, and bit, and said nothing at all. It stayed down. Avitus was so pleased that he had to go away and blow his nose.

They ate that night under the stern awning, out of the wind: salt pork from the copper, grey and stringy and soft in its fat, with biscuit broken into the broth, and Avitus's onions, sharp enough to smell over the brimstone. Felix brought the pot aft. Alana sat on a coil of cable with a blanket round her and the weather-book on her knee, and the king's paper open on the weather-book, and wrote the hold into her column for stores a line at a time. At the foot she sat a moment without writing. Then she folded the paper back, and handed it to him.

It was a clerk's list, in the chancery hand, every line the height of the one above it: what there was, and how much, and the weight against the margin. Under the clerk's last line there was a space. Under the space was a line in another hand, cramped and slow, the letters leaning on one another as they always had.

*Eat what I eat.*

He read it. Then he gave her the paper back, and ate his pork.

It tasted of its cask. He ate all of it, and wiped the bowl round with his biscuit.

"There's more," said Alana.

She held the paper to the lamp, and put the end of her pen under the foot of it. Below the line, by itself, the same hand had written one word more.

*Constancy.*

He knew it. Any boy who had grown up under the Old Hill knew it. His grandfather's ships had lain in their berths below the palace all his boyhood, masted, in their order from the hill to the mole, until the year they sailed for Thessa; and the one beside the *Concord*, the next along from the hill, had been the *Constancy*. He had last seen her berth from his father's window: a dark one, beside the first, where the *Answer* lay now with a lantern at her taffrail.

*The crown names its own.* He had said that himself, across a pot of mussels.

"Is that her?" said Alana.

"That's her."

She shut the paper into the weather-book at the day's frost, and went back to her supper.

He took the name aft. Nerva sat where he had sat going north, on the bare deck by the steering oar with his back to the sternpost, in a blanket. He was watching the helmsman. The helmsman had stopped carrying his point of larboard helm somewhere off the capital's moles, and held her now as straight as a man can hold anything with that much silence at his back.

Marcus told him.

Nerva did not answer at once. He looked out past the helmsman into the dark, where the Grey was, and moved his lips over the word, as a sentry tries the watchword over before he gives it.

"*Constancy*," he said. "Well." He settled his shoulders against the post. "She'll want it. With her step where he's put it."

All the next day Marcus kept to the rail by the mast. There was nowhere else on a galley to stand that was not in somebody's way, and he liked it there. The Grey had shut down round them an hour out of the capital and did not lift. It had no edges and no top: the mast went up into it and stopped. Sound went into it and did not come back, so that the drum in the waist seemed to beat a long way off, and a man calling from the bow might have been on another ship. The brimstone got into the biscuit. By noon his feet had stopped telling him anything, and he stamped up and down by the mast until they answered, and the rowers grinned at him over their looms.

Alana and Valeria had Avitus's cabin again. Marcus slept on deck in the lee of the mast, with a fleece under him and another over him, and woke with the outside of the top one frozen as stiff as a hide. He did not mind. He had slept in worse places drunk, and remembered fewer of them.

On the last morning the Grey began to thin. The masthead showed through it, and then the yard, and then a sky as white as milk; and in the middle of the morning the *Patience* rowed out of the last of it into clear cold air and a low sun.

Astern, the Grey lay along the sea as far as he could see either way, yellow, and did not move. Ahead the water was black and flat to the edge of sight, and empty but for themselves. They were on the cold grounds. He had rowed over that water once, in a squall, and could do nothing on it but row. He did not look at it long.

At noon they raised the Kettle: a long low smoke on the sea ahead, white against the black, and beyond it, so faint that he was not sure of it at first, a greater smoke that was Cyrene. The rowers saw it, and the stroke quickened without anybody calling for it.

Avitus took her into the Kettle slowly, on the lead, with a man in the bows swinging it and singing the water back to the helmsman. The smoke closed over them. It stank of eggs, as the Grey did; but it was warm. It lay on his face, wet, and got in at his collar. His ears came back first, and burned, and then his feet.

Valeria lifted her head from the rail and breathed it in, eggs and all, and was not sick.

Felix fetched the leather bag up from under his bench, and did not put it down again. Alana wrote into her book the hour they had come out of the Grey, and the Kettle under it. Then she shut the book on her pen, and leaned on the rail by Marcus with her shoulder against his arm; and the *Patience* felt her way through the smoke on the leadsman's song.

---

They came out of the Kettle's smoke in the dusk, and smelled Cyrene before they saw it: woodsmoke, and the hot oak of the slip-yard, and somebody frying fish.

The island followed its smell, as it always did. The steam hung over the town in a long pale bank from the slip-yard to the Baths, as over a pot just off the boil, and under it the snow lay white on the roofs and the headland and the fort. The moles were a darkness on the water. There were no lights yet.

Then there was one, low down between the moles, looking straight out at them through the gap.

"There's a light in the gap," said Avitus thickly, at his elbow. "Was that there before?"

"Since the squall."

Avitus waited, sniffing.

"It's for a drowned man," said Marcus.

"Ah." Avitus wiped his nose on his rag, and said something to the helmsman; and the *Patience* swung a little, and laid her bow on the light.

It was Prisca's lamp, in her upper window at the end of Netmakers' Row, with its back to the town.

The harbor bell began up on the sea-gate, for a ship and a friend.

Then a torch was lit at the head of the long quay, and another beside it. After them the lights came running out along the quay on both sides, one after another and faster, as fast as people can run with fire in their hands, to the seaward end. When they stopped, the long quay lay across the dark harbor between its lines of torches, and the steam off the gutters drifted through their light.

He had not known the town owned so many torches.

On the quay a drum began, slow, for the stroke. The *Patience*'s own drummer, who had beaten for her all the way from the capital, heard it and fell in with it, and she came in alongside on Cyrene's time.

Avitus stood back from the head of the plank to let him go first, and there was no way round it. Marcus went down.

Tuccius's boys were drawn up at the foot of it in their ranks, as at drill, with torches where their ash poles should have been. Kaeso was at the end of the front rank with the crown's drum, and Tuccius at their head, with his lancea grounded. Behind the rear rank, a head taller than any boy in it, stood the old elf in her grey, with a torch in her fist. She held it as if somebody had handed it to her and she had not yet decided what it was for.

"Highness," said Tuccius.

"Tuccius."

"She had you at noon. Off the Kettle." He moved his chin at Maelis. "Said you'd a man aft who didn't trust his helmsman."

Up on the deck behind him, Nerva said nothing at all.

Felix had followed him down the plank with the bag. Marcus took it from him and opened it, and took out the crown of beaks. The torchlight ran round the gilt and caught on every little beak.

"By the old law of the fleet," said Marcus, "it goes to the first man over the enemy's rail. It's yours."

Tuccius looked at it. He did not take it.

"I never got over her rail, Highness. I was on my knee on the bridge."

"That's where they went over you."

Tuccius rubbed his nose with his thumb. His boys' torches had sagged; every face in the ranks was on the crown, and the big one with the boathook had his mouth open. Tuccius took the crown, carefully, and tucked it under his arm like a helmet on parade.

"Did I say look at it?" he said to his boys, not loudly. "Torches up."

The torches went up.

Marcus went on up the quay, fast. Orso was at the head of it with the council, and the town was shouting his name from both sides, and he had never learned what to do with that except keep walking. Faces passed him in the torchlight: Rufo with the cohort at his back, nodding once, as he nodded on a deck; Pen with his weight on one hip; Oppius a head above everybody, floured to the elbow even at that hour. The net-wives leaned out of the loft windows over the Row and shouted things that made the cohort laugh. Along the steam-box wall the prisoners had got up from their supper to see, with their bowls in their hands, and the smell of it reached him through the steam: cod stewed with leeks, and Fausta's bread.

Roderic sat the grey mare at the head of the quay, by the shrine of the harbor gods.

"You're late, my boy," he said. "They've been looking at that wood since Midwinter."

There was wood. It was stacked by the shrine as high as a man, pine and driftwood and the broken staves of old casks, with a skin of snow on it that nobody had brushed off. There was another stack at the head of the slip-yard, and he could see more along the black beach, dark heaps above the surf.

"We held the fires," said Orso.

He was a big man, grey-bearded, and he said it as he said most things to the crown: as if it had cost nothing, and he would like a receipt.

"Till we knew," he said. "The council voted."

"Crown's seat voted against," said Old Baebius beside him, with his hood up over his one ear against the cold.

"Crown's seat votes against the tide," said Orso. "Well?"

"Leave granted," said Marcus.

"In his own hand?" said Roderic, from the saddle.

"In his own hand. In the Lesser Hall, at Midwinter."

"He always did write short," said Roderic.
